On 14 May 1971 the Commission Against Corruption opened an inquiry into the sale of import licences for fertilizer and motor vehicles under the Commercial Import Program, which its auditors had traced through three Saigon firms to a relative of a member of the President's staff. It was the first inquiry in seven years to reach the presidential palace, and it reached it in the year Thieu stood for re-election. The American lever that had protected the Commission since 1964, the Aid Compliance Board's power to suspend counterpart funds to a named command, importer or province, had not been used since 1969: the Mission was managing a withdrawal, and Washington would not risk a quarrel with the government it was leaving over a licence case. Commissioner Khoa's second term, from January 1970, had been confirmed with the American co-chair's concurrence; his removal still required it¹⁰³¹⁶⁸.

Thieu did not remove him. On 9 July 1971 he issued a decree placing the Commission, the Special Court's prosecutors and the Pay and Muster Verification Service under a new Inspectorate General of the Presidency, with authority to approve or suspend any inquiry touching an official of the rank of minister, general officer or province chief and above. The licence inquiry was suspended the same day. The Deputy Chief of Mission protested in writing; the Embassy did not invoke the Board. Khoa stayed in office and the Commission's work below that rank went on: the musters, the pay at the parade, the land court cases and the Phoenix audits continued through 1971 at about their earlier rate. What ended was the reach upward that had convicted a general in 1967. An anti-corruption body that had survived seven years with a patron and an American lever lost the lever first, and the patron followed his own interest¹⁶⁸.

#### ◆ Thieu's unopposed re-election
*Oct 1971 · Republic of Vietnam · historical · EV000-73*
*Initiative: neither*

In the presidential election of October 1971 Thieu ran alone. Ky was at first disqualified under an election law requiring endorsements from deputies or provincial councillors, then reinstated by the Supreme Court, and withdrew; Duong Van Minh withdrew, charging that the election was rigged. Thieu received all the valid votes, 5,971,114, and the reported turnout was 87.97 percent of 7,192,660 registered voters; valid ballots were 94.37 percent of those cast²⁴⁵. A government that had won with a third of the vote in a contested election in 1967 was now returned in a referendum on itself. The army and administration remained Thieu's, and the regime was more stable than at any time since 1963; but its claim to stand for the whole non-communist South had narrowed, at the moment American forces were leaving it to stand alone.

In this war the election runs as it did historically, with the same withdrawals, the same single candidate and the same returns. Its context is different in one respect and the same in another. Thieu's government could claim in the countryside what it could not claim on the historical course, that it had given about 800,000 families their land seven years before the election, and its province chiefs campaigned on it. But the subordination of the Commission in July had removed the one body with the standing to hear the opposition's complaints about the endorsement law, and Minh's charge that the election was rigged was heard in Saigon as a description of a government that had shut down its own inspector in the year it chose to run alone¹⁶⁸.

The colonels' petition entered the election in August, when a deputy of the Lower House read its text into the record and asked why its signers had been removed from their commands before an operation in which the army had lost so much. Minh's charge that the election was rigged was heard by the officer corps alongside it. The board went on promoting captains and majors through the campaign as if nothing had happened, which was in its way the strongest thing it ever did; the officers it promoted that autumn knew that the list above colonel was now the President's⁶³.

#### How the phase ends
At the end of 1971 the United States has about 158,000 military personnel in Vietnam, its ground combat role is nearly over, and the last US combat divisions are leaving¹⁶⁷. The RVNAF numbers more than a million men under arms, well equipped and, in the territorial forces, more present in the hamlets than ever before²³⁴. In the countryside the VC remain weaker than at any time since 1960 and weaker in the Delta than on the historical course; the land programme of 1964 has finished its work, with about 1,150,000 hectares titled, and the Land to the Tiller law of 1970 was never needed. The Commission survives below the rank of minister and general and no longer above it¹⁶⁸. These gains are real, and they are measured mostly by indicators the government itself produces.

On the frontiers the balance is the reverse. The PAVN has rebuilt its main forces, extended the Trail in Laos and Cambodia, lost Sihanoukville and replaced it, and watched the ARVN's best divisions fail in Laos without American advisers. The secret channel in Paris is deadlocked on Thieu's removal and on the PAVN's presence in the South. In Hanoi, after Lam Son 719, the Politburo has decided to stake the next year on a conventional invasion across the DMZ, from Laos into the Central Highlands and from Cambodia towards Saigon, timed to an American election year and to the near completion of the American withdrawal²⁴⁴. The question of Phase V is the one Vietnamization was built to answer: whether the South can stop a PAVN conventional offensive with American air power but without American ground troops.

The Board still ranks the candidates and the Prime Minister now chooses the loyal among the competent¹⁶⁹. The posts, the census and the district Special Branch sections stand, and the historical police has caught up with them in size; what the Republic keeps from 1964 is the chiefs' experience and four extra years of files.

The radar's ledger has made the interdiction debate honest and changed nothing it was about: the Trail is larger at the end of 1971 than ever, the Liberation Route through northeastern Cambodia is mapped and open, and the aircraft that count both will leave with the Americans¹⁷².

The ARVN's I Corps ends the phase believing, on the evidence of Lolo and Sophia, that a besieged base under American bombers holds. It is true, and it is the belief that will cost it most when the bombers go¹⁷⁶.

Phung Hoang ends 1971 with an audited count of about 63,000 neutralized since 1965 and a national file of about 230,000 names in one building in Saigon, serving the President as well as the war. About 3,500 selected advisers remain, with the territorials and the province teams more than with the divisions; the American battalions have gone home whole and their discipline has decayed on its historical course¹⁷⁰.

The board ends 1971 as the machinery of the army below colonel and nothing above it: battalions and regiments are commanded by officers it chose, for terms it sets, and the divisions and corps by the President's choices⁶³. The colonels who asked for the list back are in the schools and the staffs. The battalions that went into Laos came out of it better than the operation did.

### Mechanism

The phase runs the reforms into the limit their memorandum named, the American lever, and the limit holds exactly where it was drawn. In January 1970 the general-officer list becomes advisory; the board survives as the army's machinery below colonel because the palace has no other, and the divisions and corps return to the President. ARVN effectiveness ends the phase at two bands above history rather than one, because the battalions and regiments are the best-led the Republic ever had and the historical army's own improvement after 1968 has not caught up with eighteen-month commands chosen by board since 1965; it is two and not three because the historical gap is narrowing and because the palace's answer to the colonels took forty of the best commanders out of the field in the winter of 1970–71.

The colonels' petition is the perverse effect the memorandum named, arriving on schedule: an educated field-grade corps whose promotion is blocked at the top becomes a faction, whether it means to or not, and the palace treats it as one. It was not a coup; the evidence for that is in the absence of one and in the fact that Trieu, who refused to sign, was trusted with a regiment on the DMZ. But the palace's security services reported a plot, the President acted on the report, and the reshuffle reached I Corps six weeks before Lam Son 719.

In Laos the gain was in the battalions and the outcome was in the corps. Battalions under board commanders came down Route 9 as units more often than history's; the operation's limits, the President's instruction, Lam's divisions reporting to Saigon and five PAVN divisions decided it, and I do not adjust the ARVN's losses. The reform had made the battalions better than the operation they were sent on.

### Enemy response and initiative

The enemy's response in this phase is patience. COSVN's proselytizing among the passed-over continues, and the palace's purge of December 1970 gives it about forty new names of officers with a grievance; the record shows no agent recruited among them, and I do not invent one. Hanoi's decision of 1971 to stake the next year on a conventional invasion was its own, taken after Lam Son 719 for reasons of its own politics and the American calendar, and the officer reforms did not enter it. The PAVN in Laos met battalions that held their companies together and killed them at the landing zones all the same.

### Causal chains

**C007-4** [war-shaping] (begins in Phase I, EV000-1) The general-officer list is protected by an American aid condition, accepted in Khanh's bargain of February 1964 and enforced in the 25th Division's case in 1967 → as the aid conditions are folded into Vietnamization the lever lapses, and in January 1970 Thieu makes the list advisory and fills four division commands in the year from his own choices → about thirty colonels of the classes of 1957–59, regimental commanders who had been promised a career on merit, petition for the list's restoration, and the palace's security services report a plot → between December 1970 and January 1971 Thieu moves about forty regimental and battalion commanders out of the field, among them two of the 1st Division's regimental commanders and an Airborne brigade commander, and Lam Son 719 goes into Laos with officers who met their staffs in January, so that the reform's success produced the officers whose removal weakened the operation that tested Vietnamization.

### Downstream

The board enters 1972 as the army's machinery below colonel and nothing above it; the 3rd Division, formed in 1971, has battalion commanders chosen by the board and a regiment under Trieu, whom the palace's report had cleared; the colonels who asked for the list back are in the schools and the staffs, and some of them write the staff study of 1974. Chapter P4 carries the change in three events and its overview and ending.

### Clock

The reforms give Washington a marginally better case for Vietnamization and change none of its timing. The ARVN's battalions in Cambodia and in Laos were better than history's, and the pictures from Route 9 were the same pictures; Nixon's claim of 7 April that Vietnamization had succeeded was neither more nor less believed. The colonels' petition, read into the record of the Lower House in August 1971, reached the American press as one story among many about Thieu's election. The withdrawal ran on its historical schedule, and the clock was by then measured in Congress's appropriations, which an army's promotion system does not reach.

## §4.P5 Phase V — The Easter Offensive and Linebacker (Jan 1972 – Dec 1972)

### Chronicle

*End of phase against the historical state: ENEMY.engagement_control -1 (old band ≈ -4..-2) · FIND.main_force +1 (old band ≈ +2..+4) · ARVN.effectiveness +1 (old band ≈ +2..+4) · ARVN.self_sufficiency -1 (old band ≈ -4..-2).*

1972 is the test of Vietnamization, and the answer it gives is exact: the Republic of Vietnam can stop a full PAVN conventional offensive with American air power and American advisers, and cannot do it without them. On 30 March the PAVN opens the largest offensive of the war, called the Nguyen Hue Offensive in Hanoi and the Easter Offensive in the West, with about fourteen divisions and hundreds of tanks on three main fronts: across the DMZ into Quang Tri, from Laos and Cambodia into the Central Highlands at Kontum, and from Cambodia down Route 13 at An Loc towards Saigon. US ground strength has fallen to about 69,000 men, few of them combat troops, and is on its way to 27,000²⁴⁶. US air power returns in strength: B-52s and tactical aircraft over the battlefields of the South, and, for the first time since 1968, a bombing campaign against the North without most of Rolling Thunder's restrictions, with the mining of Haiphong.

The shape of the phase is a near-collapse in the spring and a recovery in the summer. Quang Tri Province falls on 1–2 May; Kontum and An Loc are besieged and held by narrow margins and B-52s; and by the autumn the ARVN, under better commanders, has retaken Quang Tri City. The PAVN loses most of the armour it committed and, by most estimates, about 100,000 men, killed and wounded, while keeping about a tenth of the South's territory, mostly thinly populated border regions in the west. The offensive creates almost a million refugees and kills more than 25,000 civilians²⁴⁶. Its political result is the one Hanoi needed: a settlement on terms that leave PAVN troops in the South. The year ends with the October draft agreement, Thieu's refusal of it, and the eleven days of Linebacker II.

In this war the armour of the Easter Offensive is seen before it crosses the DMZ and is struck by radar-bombing B-52s through the cloud that grounded the fighter-bombers; Linebacker chooses from target folders seven years in the making and counts the effect of the mining on the North's own roads; and at An Loc the B-52 boxes of 10–12 May fall on assembly areas the radar shows. Quang Tri still falls, An Loc and Kontum still hold, and the lessons both sides draw are the historical ones.

The B-52J's year is this one. Freedom Train drops the Thanh Hoa bridge on its first night; the siege box breaks the penetration of An Loc on 12 May and the assault on Kontum short of the wire, and Kontum is secure five days early; and Linebacker II loses about six bombers rather than fifteen and does not strike Bach Mai or Kham Thien Street. Quang Tri still falls, and the terms of January are the terms of October.

The officer reforms meet the offensive at regiment and battalion, and the offensive is decided above them. On the DMZ front the 3rd Division's battalions, under commanders the board chose, give ground in better order than history's, and the 56th Regiment's commander, a board-chosen officer with a hero's record from Hue, surrenders Camp Carroll all the same. At Quang Tri the contradictory orders of the corps and the division are the historical orders, and the division still breaks, but one of its regiments holds the road south open long enough to bring out its column. At Tan Canh more of the 22nd Division gets out, and Kontum and An Loc are held as before. The army fights the year as it was taught to fight, with American air at the centre of every plan, and depends on it more for having learned to use it well.

#### ◆ The Easter Offensive opens: the DMZ front
*30 Mar – 2 Apr 1972 · Quang Tri Province, from the DMZ to the Cua Viet and Camp Carroll · changed from history · EV000-74*
*Initiative: enemy*

The armour was seen before it crossed. From 12 March the Gulf watch, standing off the Quang Tri coast outside the SA-2s that the North had moved into and just north of the DMZ, tracked tracked-vehicle columns moving south at night on Route 1A and Route 101 from Vinh Linh toward the Ben Hai, and the panhandle watch tracked others moving east along Route 9 in Laos toward Khe Sanh. By 25 March the J-2 count of armoured vehicles in assembly areas within 20 kilometres of the DMZ was about 90–120, and MACV warned I Corps on 27 March of an armoured attack across the demarcation line within days. Lieutenant General Lam, the I Corps commander, expected the main blow from the west, as most of the American and Vietnamese staffs did; the warning reached the 3rd Division, and the rotation of its regiments between firebases, which history records as under way when the attack fell, went ahead in part¹⁷².

At midday on 30 March 1972 PAVN artillery, including 130mm guns that outranged anything the ARVN had, opened on the firebases strung along the DMZ, and the 304th and 308th Divisions with armoured regiments attacked across the demarcation line, while the 324B came in from the A Shau and the west. In all, PAVN forces on the northern front were supported by more than 100 tanks, T-54s, T-34s and PT-76s. The front was held by the ARVN 3rd Division under Brigadier General Vu Van Giai, a new division formed in late 1971 from mixed and unsteady units, with the 147th and 258th Vietnamese Marine Brigades under its control²⁴⁷. Low cloud kept most air support away for the first days²⁴⁶. It did not keep the radar away. The B-52s, which bombed by radar in any weather, were put on the crossing points of the Ben Hai and the roads south of Gio Linh where the Gulf watch showed the armour moving, and on 31 March and 1 April they struck three columns in the open; by our own estimate, about 20–30 armoured vehicles were destroyed or disabled before they reached the firebases¹⁷².

The firebases fell back or were overrun all the same. On 2 April the 56th Regiment at Camp Carroll, under Lieutenant Colonel Pham Van Dinh, surrendered with about 1,500 men. That afternoon at Dong Ha, Captain John Ripley, US adviser to the 3rd Vietnamese Marine Battalion, spent about three hours under fire placing charges on the highway bridge over the Cua Viet, which was blown at 16:30 as PAVN tanks approached; the Marines held the river line²⁴⁷. The PAVN halted to regroup and bring up supplies, and the 3rd Division, under fire from guns it could not reach, settled into a defence of Quang Tri City along the Thach Han and Cua Viet rivers. The radar had seen the tanks; it had not made the 3rd Division's regiments steady or the 56th's commander stay.

The officer reforms did not make the 3rd Division steady; nothing done in six months could have. Its regiments had been formed in late 1971 from units of the 1st Division, Regional Forces and men returned from desertion, and its battalion commanders, though chosen by the board, had held their battalions for less than six months when the guns opened. What they changed was the manner of the withdrawal from the DMZ outposts on 1–2 April. The 57th Regiment, given to Colonel Bui Quang Trieu in 1971, came back from the firebases along Route 1 toward Dong Ha by bounds, a battalion holding while the next passed through it, with its 105s towed out and its wounded on its own trucks, and reached the Cua Viet line as a regiment; on the historical course it arrived as a crowd among the refugees. Lieutenant Colonel Pham Van Dinh, who had commanded the 56th since 1971 from the board's list on the strength of his battalion's fight at the Hue Citadel in 1968, surrendered Camp Carroll on 2 April as he did historically²⁴⁷⁶³. The board had chosen him for what he had done, and could not choose for what he would do.

#### Freedom Train, Pocket Money and Linebacker
*5 Apr – 23 Oct 1972 · North Vietnam, Haiphong harbour and the approaches to the DMZ · changed from history · EV000-75*
*Initiative: allied*

On 5 April Nixon authorized strikes north of the 20th parallel, Operation Freedom Train, and on 10 April B-52s struck petroleum storage near Vinh²⁴⁸. In this war the first B-52J strike of Freedom Train went to the folder the cell had kept since 1964: on the night of 10 April twelve Js put 240 GBU-31s on the Ham Rong bridge, the Thanh Hoa rail yards and the fuel tanks at Vinh, in cloud, from about twelve miles out, and the Dragon's Jaw fell into the Song Ma seven years and seven days after the request to drop it had been refused. The laser-guided bombs of 27 April and 13 May had nothing left there to do; on 10–11 May the Paul Doumer bridge at Hanoi was dropped by F-4s with laser-guided bombs, as it was historically, because the restricted zone around Hanoi stayed closed to the B-52 until December²⁰⁰¹⁷⁶.

On 8 May (9 May in Vietnam) Nixon announced Operation Pocket Money, the mining of Haiphong and the other ports. Navy aircraft from the *Coral Sea* laid the first mines in the Haiphong channel, as they did historically; here the B-52Js laid the secondary fields the same night, about 900 Mk 64 and Mk 65 Quickstrikes in the approaches to Hon Gai, Cam Pha, Thanh Hoa, Vinh and Dong Hoi and in the inland waterways south of Haiphong, including about 200 Quickstrike-ERs released more than forty miles out¹⁷⁶. The mines were activated after five days, and about 11,000 more were sown in the secondary harbours and waterways over the following weeks²⁴⁸. The mining of the northern ports, prohibited since 1965, closed the North's main entry point for Soviet supplies by sea; 27 ships stayed in Haiphong¹⁷⁷. The Gulf watch saw it close, and from June saw the trucks massing at the Chinese border crossings at Dong Dang and Lao Cai to replace the sea¹⁷².

Linebacker, from 10 May to 23 October, opened the North to bombing with targets chosen by the commanders rather than in Washington. On its first day, in 414 sorties, the heaviest air-to-air fighting of the war took place, and Lieutenant Randy Cunningham and Lieutenant (jg) William Driscoll became the first American aces of the war. In this war the commanders chose from folders seven years in the making, and the Js struck the bridges, rail yards and depots between Hanoi and the DMZ from the first week, about 1,100 J sorties with GBU-31s against fixed targets outside the Hanoi restricted zone by October. Linebacker flew 41,653 missions and dropped 155,548 tons, and lost 134 aircraft, 104 in combat; I estimate that the electronic order of battle and the J's work on fixed targets, which kept fighter-bombers off many of the most heavily defended bridges, saved perhaps 15–25 of those losses, and I hold the estimate loosely. Its effect was felt at the front: by a PAVN history only 30 percent of planned supplies reached its front-line units in May and June, the PAVN shelling of the South fell by half between 9 May and 1 June, and by September imports were 35–50 percent below the May level. The radar's count of southbound traffic below Vinh in July was about 35 percent of the March level, against about 40 percent with the historical bombers, a difference I attribute to the bridges falling in the first week rather than the first month¹⁷²¹⁷⁶. The Soviet Union and China denounced the mining and did nothing; the Nixon–Brezhnev summit in Moscow went ahead from 22 May²⁴⁸.

#### Loc Ninh and the siege of An Loc
*7 Apr – 18 Jun 1972 · Loc Ninh and An Loc, Binh Long Province, on Route 13 · changed from history · EV000-76*
*Initiative: enemy*

On 5 April the 5th Division of the PAVN B-2 Front attacked Loc Ninh, on Route 13 near the Cambodian border, and overran it on the afternoon of 7 April; the 9th Division took the Quan Loi base camp that evening, and the 7th Division cut Route 13 south of An Loc at the Tau O bridge with its 141st, 165th and 209th Regiments. An Loc, the provincial capital 100 km north of Saigon, was cut off by 8 April. It was held by the ARVN 5th Division under Brigadier General Le Van Hung, with the 3rd and 81st Ranger Groups, the 1st Airborne Brigade lifted in after failing to break through at Tau O, and the Regional Forces, Popular Forces and People's Self-Defence Force of Binh Long Province, all under III Corps (Lieutenant General Nguyen Van Minh) and advised by the American regional assistance command under Major General James Hollingsworth²⁴⁹.

From mid-April a Global Hawk stood over Binh Long and watched the roads out of Cambodia¹⁷². From 15 April it was given the town itself, every night, and Sorrell's detachment at Long Binh measured what it saw: the PAVN's gun positions in the rubber, the bunkers dug into the ruined streets on the town's northern edge, the tank harbours, and the trenches the 9th Division pushed toward the perimeter each night. The 9th Division assaulted on 13 April with tanks; in that first attack the defenders destroyed tanks with M72 LAWs at close range, and tank-killing teams were formed in every battalion. From 16 April cells of Js came over An Loc every two hours, each with thirty to forty GBU-38s aimed at positions measured that night, the nearest about 400 metres from Hung's lines; by early May the PAVN's guns fired from the rubber a few rounds at a time and moved, and the town's shelling, which historically reached thousands of rounds on the worst days, came at perhaps two-thirds of that weight. The town survived on airdrops, taken over by USAF C-130s on 19 April once anti-aircraft fire made helicopter landings impossible²⁴⁹; the guns around the drop zone were measured and struck each night, and the airdrops of May landed inside the perimeter more often than they had in April¹⁷⁶.

The decisive assault came on 11 May. Historically B-52s struck one mission every 55 minutes for 30 hours against it, with AC-119 and AC-130E gunships, A-37s and AH-1 Cobras. Here the assault's assembly was on the radar from the evening of 10 May, as tanks and trucks moved up to the rubber north and east of the town, and Hollingsworth's targeteers put the night's first cells on the assembly areas the radar showed¹⁷². The difference the J made was in the hours after the attack broke into the town. Historically the B-52 carpets could not fall on the PAVN units inside the northern part of An Loc without falling on the defenders; here the cell measured the buildings and bunkers the attackers had taken, and cells of Js put GBU-38s on them 300–400 metres from the ARVN line, one aimpoint to a bomb, through the morning of 12 May. The 9th Division's penetration in the north was destroyed by noon on 12 May, and the tanks that had come in with it burned in the streets¹⁷⁶. The 21st Division fought up Route 13 from Lai Khe. The PAVN was driven from the town by 12 June and III Corps declared the siege over on 18 June²⁴⁹²⁴⁶.

US estimates of PAVN losses are 10,000 killed and 15,000 wounded and 36 tanks; Vietnamese figures are about 2,000 killed and 5,000 wounded. I estimate that the GBU-38 cells added to the PAVN's losses perhaps a fifth beyond the historical, and that the 5th Division and its attached units lost perhaps a fifth fewer killed than the historical garrison, and I do not alter the reported figures, which already disagree by a factor of five. Brigadier General Richard Tallman, the deputy American commander, was mortally wounded by artillery on 9 July²⁴⁹. An Loc lay in ruins. It became the symbol of an ARVN that could hold if the B-52s came; in this war, of an ARVN that could hold if the B-52s came, the Americans could see where to send them, and an American cell could turn what they saw into coordinates¹⁷⁶.

#### ◆ Tan Canh, Dak To and the battle of Kontum
*24 Apr – 9 Jun 1972 · Tan Canh, Dak To, Ben Het and Kontum city, Kontum Province · changed from history · EV000-77*
*Initiative: enemy*

In the Central Highlands the PAVN 320th Division began pressing the firebases on Rocket Ridge in early April, and on 24 April the 2nd Division's 1st and 66th Regiments, with the D10 Sapper Battalion and tanks, overran the ARVN bases at Tan Canh and Dak To II, held by the 42nd and 47th Regiments of the 22nd Division; the division "ceased to exist as a fighting unit", and its commander, Colonel Le Duc Dat, disappeared. Tan Canh fell in a day, to tanks and infantry already inside the wire before dawn, and nothing in the J reached a fight of that kind¹⁷⁶. The road to Kontum lay open. The PAVN paused for three weeks. John Paul Vann, the civilian director of the II Corps advisory effort, effectively directed the defence; he had Colonel Ly Tong Ba of the 23rd Division put in command of Kontum, and on 10 May Major General Nguyen Van Toan replaced Ngo Du at II Corps²⁴⁶. On 9 May at Ben Het, ARVN Rangers destroyed three PT-76s with BGM-71 TOW missiles, among the first combat uses of the weapon, and TOW-armed helicopters followed²⁵⁰.

The three weeks were the B3 Front's to lose, and they were the time the siege box needed. From 26 April a Global Hawk was given to Kontum every night, and Vann had a detachment of the coordinate cell flown to the city's airfield on 28 April. By 13 May it had measured about 600 positions in the arc north and west of the city: the 320th Division's assembly areas along the Krong Poko, gun pits on the ridges, the trench lines dug toward the 23rd Division's perimeter, and the tank harbours of the 203rd Armored Regiment. Cells of Js struck the measured positions from 2 May, and by the eve of the assault about a third of the guns the PAVN had brought to Kontum had been destroyed or abandoned in their pits¹⁷⁶.

The main assault on Kontum came on 14 May, by the 320th Division's 48th, 52nd and 64th Regiments and the 2nd Division²⁴⁶. B-52 strikes on 14 May and on the night of 16–17 May fell close to the ARVN lines and broke the momentum of the attack. Here the cells put GBU-38s on the PAVN regiments' measured start lines through the night of 13–14 May, and on 14 May the attacking battalions came forward through ground that had been struck one position at a time; the 48th Regiment's assault on the northern perimeter was broken short of the wire, and the penetration on the city's northern edge, which historically took two weeks to clear, was held to a few blocks. From 26 May to 3 June the ARVN cleared the city bunker by bunker, with the cell putting GBU-38s on bunkers 300 metres from Ba's lines, and on 4 June Ba, now a brigadier general, declared Kontum secure, five days earlier than historically. On 9 June Vann's helicopter flew into a hillside in low cloud and he was killed; a Vietnamese account says it was shot down. US estimates put PAVN casualties in the Kontum battle at about 4,000 and in the Highlands campaign at 20,000–40,000, figures with no better basis than the body counts of earlier years²⁵⁰. I estimate that the 23rd Division lost perhaps a quarter fewer killed than historically, and I do not adjust the PAVN's figures¹⁷⁶.

At Tan Canh the board's battalion commanders could not hold a position whose wire the tanks were inside before dawn, and did not; what changed was how many of their men got out. The 42nd Regiment's battalions broke out of Tan Canh on 24 April in their companies, not as individuals, and about a third more of the regiment's men reached Kontum over the following week than on the historical course. The 22nd Division's command did not change; its commander had been the palace's choice after the list became advisory, and his disappearance is the historical record's²⁵⁰⁶³. Kontum was held by the 23rd Division under Ba, whose regiments were commanded by board officers, and it was held as told above, by the siege box and the infantry together.

#### ◆ The fall of Quang Tri
*27 Apr – 2 May 1972 · Quang Tri City and Highway 1 south to the My Chanh River · changed from history · EV000-78*
*Initiative: enemy*

On 27 April the PAVN renewed its attack on the 3rd Division's defence of Quang Tri City. Contradictory orders from the I Corps commander, Lieutenant General Hoang Xuan Lam, and from Giai left units moving in opposite directions, and on 1 May Giai ordered the city abandoned. The division broke. Soldiers and refugees fled south on Highway 1, and PAVN artillery fired on the column, killing roughly 2,000 soldiers and civilians on the road²⁴⁷. Quang Tri Province was lost by 2 May; the Marines who held at the My Chanh River were the one coherent force left north of Hue. On 2 May Thieu relieved Lam and gave I Corps to Lieutenant General Ngo Quang Truong, who stopped the rout, restored order in Hue and held the My Chanh line with the Marine and Airborne Divisions and the 1st Division. Giai was later court-martialled and sentenced to five years' imprisonment²⁴⁶. The fall of Quang Tri was the first loss of a province to a PAVN conventional offensive, and Truong's recovery the clearest demonstration of what leadership was worth in the ARVN.

In this war the orders were the same and one regiment's conduct was not. When Giai ordered the city abandoned on 1 May, the 57th Regiment under Colonel Bui Quang Trieu was holding the bridges over the Thach Han south of the city, and Trieu held them until about 1500 on 1 May, three hours after his orders allowed, while the division's rear echelons and about 20,000 refugees crossed; he then brought the regiment south on Highway 1 behind the column with its two battalions astride the road and the third on the dunes to the east, where the PAVN artillery's observers had been placed historically. The column was still shelled. I put the dead on the road at about 1,500 rather than about 2,000, and the 3rd Division's losses in men who simply disappeared at about a quarter fewer, judgments I hold loosely. The fall of Quang Tri was not prevented and could not have been at that level; it was lost by the corps and the division, by orders that sent units in opposite directions, and Truong's recovery of the line in May was the recovery of a commander the list had named in 1966 and the palace had chosen in 1972 when it had no other⁶³.

#### The recapture of Quang Tri City
*28 Jun – 16 Sep 1972 · Quang Tri Province south of the Thach Han River and the Quang Tri Citadel · historical · EV000-79*
On 28 June Truong opened the counter-offensive, Lam Son 72, with the Airborne Division and the Marine Division under Brigadier General Bui The Lan, whose 147th, 258th and 369th Marine Brigades did most of the fighting for the city. Against them were the 304th, 308th, 320B and 325th Divisions with the 312th identified. US forces provided fire and movement but no ground troops: in July alone, 5,461 tactical sorties and 2,054 B-52 strikes; US Marine helicopters of HMM-164 and HMM-165 lifted the Vietnamese Marines into landing zones, losing several to SA-7 missiles; and the 9th Marine Amphibious Brigade and the Seventh Fleet staged amphibious feints. The PAVN held the Quang Tri Citadel, its walls about 15 feet high, with interlocking tunnels and positions; the Marines breached the southeast wall on 10–11 September, held the Citadel by the late afternoon of 15 September, and raised the flag over the west gate on 16 September after 138 days of occupation. The city was rubble²⁵¹.

The Marine Division lost 977 killed of 3,658 casualties, and the Airborne Division's 2nd Brigade was badly depleted. South Vietnamese claims are 8,135 PAVN killed; Hanoi's are 4,000–10,000. About 20 Americans were killed. The province north of the Thach Han remained in PAVN hands, and both sides were too exhausted to move further²⁵¹. The battle proved that the ARVN's best divisions, under a good commander and with unlimited American fire support, could defeat the PAVN in a set-piece battle. Every one of those conditions was American or rare.

#### The last American ground combat units leave
*Aug 1972 · South Vietnam · historical · EV000-80*
In August 1972 the last US ground combat battalion in South Vietnam, the 3rd Battalion, 21st Infantry of the 196th Brigade, stood down at Da Nang and left the country. American advisers, aviation units, the air and naval forces in and around Vietnam, and a shrinking logistics command remained, and US strength fell to about 24,000 by the end of the year. US deaths by hostile action fell to 300 in 1972¹⁶⁷. The withdrawal begun at Midway in 1969 had been completed for the ground war during the largest PAVN offensive of the conflict, and the defence of the South on the ground had become entirely the RVNAF's. US air power, at its 1972 peak, was the part of the American commitment that remained.

#### The October agreement and Thieu's refusal
*8 Oct – 13 Dec 1972 · Paris, Saigon and Washington · historical · EV000-81*
On 8 October Le Duc Tho presented Kissinger in Paris with a draft that dropped Hanoi's demand for Thieu's removal: a ceasefire in place, the withdrawal of all US forces within 60 days, the return of prisoners, the Thieu government to remain alongside the PRG pending a political settlement through a council of reconciliation, and no requirement for PAVN forces to leave the South. Kissinger accepted it as the basis for an agreement. Thieu rejected it: it left the PAVN in his country and gave the PRG standing, and he had not been consulted in its drafting. On 26 October Kissinger announced that "peace is at hand". On 7 November Nixon was re-elected, carrying 49 states. Washington then sent Saigon a mass of equipment under Operation Enhance Plus, aircraft, armour and artillery, to be in the country before a ceasefire froze replacement at one-for-one, and Nixon gave Thieu private written assurances that the United States would respond with full force to a major violation²³¹.

In November and December Kissinger brought Saigon's amendments to Paris, Hanoi hardened its own terms in reply, and the main round of talks broke down on 13 December. Nixon, concerned that the new Congress in January would cut off the war's funding, gave Hanoi an ultimatum²⁵².

#### Linebacker II
*18–29 Dec 1972 · Hanoi, Haiphong and their surroundings · changed from history · EV000-82*
*Initiative: allied*

On 18 December B-52s attacked Hanoi and Haiphong for the first time in the war. In eleven days, with a 36-hour pause at Christmas, 741 B-52 sorties were dispatched and 729 completed against rail yards at Kinh No, Lang Dang and Yen Vien, power plants including Thai Nguyen, petroleum storage, airfields and SAM sites, and fighter-bombers added about 5,000 tons. In this war the 729 were J sorties, and about a third of them carried twenty GBU-31s each against measured aimpoints in the rail yards, the power plants and the SAM sites the U-2S had kept on the cell's board since 1965; the rest carried the J's unguided load, and the tonnage was about 11,000 rather than about 15,237²⁵²¹⁷⁶. The Js released their guided weapons from about twelve miles out and above the clouds that covered Hanoi on most nights; the North's SA-2 batteries still reached them, because the missile's envelope is longer than the bomb's glide¹⁷⁷.

The first three nights' routes and timings were set by SAC headquarters, as they were historically, and the North's missile crews learned them as they did historically. North Vietnam fired 266 SA-2s over the eleven days. The United States acknowledged 15 B-52s shot down historically; here I estimate six Js lost, about two-fifths of the historical figure, from the J's later electronic-warfare suite and its higher, faster release, and from the GBU-31s that destroyed the SA-2 sites at Kinh No and Phuc Yen on the fourth night rather than the eighth. Of the crews in the lost Js, about thirteen men were killed or missing and about twelve captured, against 33 killed or missing and 33 captured historically, with the five-man crew accounting for part of the difference¹⁷⁶. 12 tactical aircraft were lost²⁵². By the last nights US commanders judged the North's SA-2 stocks nearly exhausted and its air defences largely suppressed.

North Vietnam's official count of civilian dead is 1,624, 1,328 in Hanoi and 306 in Haiphong. The Bach Mai hospital was struck historically on 22 December by bombs aimed at the Bach Mai airfield and its storage, and on 26 December bombs on Kham Thien Street, aimed at the rail yards, killed 278 by Hanoi's count. Here the airfield and the rail yards were struck with GBU-31s, and neither the hospital nor Kham Thien Street was hit; unguided bombs from the other cells still fell outside the targets, and I estimate the civilian dead at about 900–1,200, a judgment and not a count. About 80 percent of the North's electrical generating capacity was put out of action. On 30 December Nixon halted the bombing north of the 20th parallel, and Hanoi agreed to resume talks, which reopened on 9 January 1973. Whether the bombing forced Hanoi back to terms or merely covered the American acceptance of terms close to October's is disputed; Le Duan later said it had "completely obliterated our economic foundation"²⁵². In this war it carried fewer American prisoners into the negotiation and fewer pictures of Bach Mai into the American press, and the terms were the same¹⁷⁶.

#### How the phase ends
At the end of 1972 the war is on the verge of a settlement that both governments in Vietnam regard as provisional. The Easter Offensive has failed to destroy the ARVN or the Thieu government, at a cost to the PAVN of most of the armour it committed (by one count 134 T-54s, 56 PT-76s and 60 T-34s) and about 100,000 men. The ARVN has held at An Loc and Kontum and retaken Quang Tri City, but has lost Quang Tri north of the Thach Han, the western Highlands and the border areas of III Corps, and its losses are given variously from about 10,000 killed to about 39,000 dead in the period. Almost a million South Vietnamese have been made refugees, about 600,000 of them in government camps²⁴⁶.

The lesson both sides draw is the same. The ARVN held where American air power was decisive, at An Loc and Kontum, and where American advisers coordinated it; it broke where its leadership failed, at Quang Tri and Tan Canh. The settlement about to be signed removes the American advisers and the American air power and leaves the PAVN in the South, while the North's air defences and economy recover. Hanoi needs only to rebuild, wait for the Americans to be gone, and choose its time.

The aircraft are still flying at the end of 1972, still American, and under the agreement about to be signed they will soon have no American strike force to cue. The Republic keeps nothing of them: no aircraft, no exploitation cells, no practice in using either.

The ARVN has held An Loc and Kontum more cheaply than history's, and has learned more completely that it holds besieged towns with American bombers measured onto the enemy by American analysts. The agreement about to be signed takes both away. The Republic enters 1973 more confident than history's and more dependent¹⁷⁶.

The ARVN's regiments and battalions have fought the offensive better than history's, and its divisions and corps no better: Quang Tri was lost by orders, and the column on Highway 1 was saved in part by a regiment. It has learned, from An Loc and Kontum, to use American air with an educated staff's precision, and it has no air of its own that its plans can rest on⁶³.

### Mechanism

The offensive meets the reforms at regiment and battalion and is decided above them, which is the memorandum's own finding that the binding constraint on ARVN leadership had moved up to division and corps. On the DMZ the 57th Regiment came back from the firebases by bounds and reached the Cua Viet line as a regiment, and the 56th's board-chosen commander surrendered Camp Carroll as he did historically. At Quang Tri the corps and the division gave the historical orders, the city was abandoned on 1 May, and one regiment held the Thach Han bridges three hours past its orders; perhaps 1,500 rather than 2,000 died on the road⁶³. At Tan Canh about a third more of the 42nd Regiment reached Kontum. I move ARVN effectiveness at the end of the phase from the historical state to one band above it, for regiments and battalions that fought better through a defeat and a recovery that were both decided by generals.

Truong's recovery of the line in May is the reforms' most visible success of the war and the clearest picture of their limit. He was the list's first name for the 1st Division in 1966, chosen by a Directory that needed the Americans' trust; he was given I Corps in 1972 by a palace that had no one else. The list found him. Only defeat put him where he was needed.

The counter-directional finding is at work in every battle of the year. The ARVN's officers were educated on curricula that put American fire at the centre of the infantry's plan, and at An Loc and Kontum they used the B-52 and the siege box with an educated staff's precision; that is why the cities held as they did. The same education made the army's plans more dependent on that fire than history's, in the year the agreement that would remove it was being negotiated.

### Enemy response and initiative

The PAVN's plan for 1972 took no account of the board and needed none: it attacked with fourteen divisions and tanks where the ARVN's corps commands were weakest, and the corps commands were the part of the army the reforms had not reached. Hanoi's choice of the DMZ front against a division formed six months earlier was a judgment about divisions, not battalions, and it was right. Where the PAVN met board-led battalions in the open, as on Route 1 south of Dong Ha, it found them harder to break and broke the division around them.

### Causal chains

**Chain review.** C006-4, from memorandum 006, ran from the eighteen-month selected advisers through their honest reports and the cutting of the division advisory teams first, to a war of 1972 and 1975 decided by divisions that were the least advised part of the RVNAF. Tested against the war as now written, it resolves as predicted at its end and differently on its way there. The divisions were the least advised and also, now, the least changed by the officer reforms; the regiments and battalions inside them, which the advisers had left first, were the part of the division the board had improved most. The chain's terminal holds, because 1972 was decided by the division and corps commands, and the reason it holds is now doubled: the advisers left the divisions, and the lists never reached them.

### Downstream

The army enters 1973 with regiments and battalions that fought the offensive better than history's, a corps in I Corps under the list's best general, and a doctrine built around American fire that the agreement about to be signed takes away; Trieu goes to the Dalat faculty after Quang Tri.

### Clock

The offensive's political effect is the historical one: Nixon's position in the election was strengthened by the ARVN's survival and the mining, not by anything a promotion board did. A smaller rout on Highway 1 made fewer photographs, and the October terms were the terms of history. The clock in 1972 was already a matter of the agreement's date, and the reforms did not move it.

## §4.P6 Phase VI — Paris, the endgame, and 1975 (Jan 1973 – Dec 1975)

### Chronicle

*End of phase against the historical state: as historical on every headline variable.*

Phase VI is the war without the Americans, and the end of the Republic of Vietnam. The Paris Agreement of January 1973 takes US forces out and returns the prisoners; it leaves the PAVN in the South, the Thieu government in Saigon, and the war to be decided between them. The ceasefire never holds. In 1973 Saigon is on the offensive, recovering hamlets and roads, while the North rebuilds; in 1974 the two are evenly matched, and the ARVN, short of ammunition, fuel and spare parts as American aid falls, bleeds in a war of attrition it cannot sustain; in January 1975 the North tests whether the United States will respond to the seizure of a province, and when it does not, it commits everything²⁵³.

The shape of the phase is three slow years and seven fast weeks. The constraints that bind the American role are now law, not policy: the Case–Church amendment ends all US combat activity in Indochina from 15 August 1973, the War Powers Resolution limits the President's power to send forces back, and Congress cuts military aid. Watergate removes the President who gave Thieu his assurances. In Hanoi, the 21st Plenum of October 1973 returns to revolutionary violence, and the PAVN builds a road network east of the mountains, a fuel pipeline into the South and an army of corps. In Saigon, the economy, hit by the oil shock and the departure of the Americans whose spending had sustained it, and a government that has narrowed its base, face the offensive of 1975 with an army spread thin across the whole country. When the blow falls at Ban Me Thuot in March, Thieu's decision to abandon the Highlands turns a defeat into a rout, and in eight weeks the Republic falls. The PAVN enters Saigon on 30 April 1975.

In this war the army that fights the last three years has been built for ten years around the belt-fed gun and the abundance of American ammunition, and the ration of 1974 finds it more dependent on volume than the historical one. It fights the attrition of 1974 and the stand at Xuan Loc with better weapons than its enemy and too few rounds to use them, and the hoards it builds against the ration are lost with I Corps.

The bombers leave in August 1973 without bombing Neak Luong, and the coordinate cell is disbanded with nothing in the VNAF to send coordinates to. The ARVN fights 1974 and the defence of Phuoc Long with plans written for an air force that no longer comes, and loses Thuong Duc and Phuoc Long as history did, with its officers knowing exactly what they lack.

Phung Hoang outlives the Americans as a Vietnamese programme and helps the Republic's offensive of 1973 in the Delta; its national file, consolidated in Saigon since 1971, is taken intact on 30 April 1975 with the files of Da Nang a month earlier, and it serves the victors' security service as the most complete list of their enemies they will ever hold. The advisers are gone in March 1973, and nothing of their eighteen months survives them but the counterparts who remember them, who are on the lists.

The officer reforms leave the Republic in its last three years an officer corps that knows precisely what it lacks. Its battalion and regimental commanders, educated on a doctrine built around American fire, plan the battles of 1974 for a weight of fire the ration no longer allows. Its staff college writes, in November 1974, the only serious plan the Republic ever had for a defence on reduced resources, and the President takes its conclusion and not its method in March 1975. At Xuan Loc a well-led division holds for twelve days, as history's did. Saigon falls on 30 April.

#### The Paris Agreement
*27 Jan 1973 · Paris · historical · EV000-83*
On 27 January 1973 the Agreement on Ending the War and Restoring Peace in Vietnam was signed in Paris by the United States, the Democratic Republic of Vietnam, the Republic of Vietnam and the Provisional Revolutionary Government. A ceasefire in place took effect on 28 January Saigon time. The United States and its allies would withdraw all forces within 60 days, and prisoners would be exchanged in the same period. Nothing required the PAVN to leave the South, where by US estimates about 150,000 of its troops remained. The Thieu government remained, and a National Council of National Reconciliation and Concord was to arrange elections, which it never did. An International Commission of Control and Supervision of Canadian, Hungarian, Indonesian and Polish personnel, and a Four-Party Joint Military Commission, were to supervise the ceasefire, and both sides could replace worn-out armaments one for one. Thieu signed under American pressure, with Nixon's written promises of continued aid and of a forceful response to a major violation²³¹. The agreement was close to the terms of October 1972.

#### The ceasefire that never held
*28 Jan – Dec 1973 · The countryside of South Vietnam · changed from history · EV000-84*
*Initiative: allied*
In the last days before the ceasefire both sides grabbed for hamlets and roads, raising flags to claim territory, the "war of the flags"; the ARVN recovered most of what the PAVN and VC had seized. Fighting resumed in earnest by March²³¹. Through most of 1973 the Saigon government was on the offensive: the Communists, rebuilding supply lines and wary of provoking the return of American air power, largely stayed on the defensive, while the ARVN and territorial forces pushed into PRG-held areas. Both sides violated the agreement from the start; the ICCS could not stop it, and Canada left the commission in July 1973. From late 1973 the PAVN grew bolder as the American political system turned on itself, and by early 1974 the two sides fought roughly on equal terms²⁵³.

In this war the Republic's offensive of 1973 had one instrument the historical one lacked: district centres nine years old in the Delta, with files the American departure did not take with it. When the American advisers left the centres in March 1973, the centres stayed, under the police statute, as they had always been; the Phung Hoang name was retired in December 1972, as historically, and the work went on under the Special Branch. In the "war of the flags" of January–February and the push into PRG-held hamlets that followed, the IV Corps provinces moved first into villages whose infrastructure the centres had charted, and the Front's district committees in Chuong Thien, Vinh Binh and Kien Hoa lost about a fifth of their members in 1973, about twice the historical rate¹⁷⁰. It was a gain in the hamlets and not on the frontiers, and it changed nothing in the balance of 1974, when the PAVN's divisions decided what the hamlets were worth.

#### Operation Homecoming and the last American troops
*12 Feb – 29 Mar 1973 · Hanoi, Clark Air Base and Saigon · historical · EV000-85*
On 12 February 1973 the first American prisoners were flown out of Hanoi to Clark Air Base in the Philippines. By 29 March, 566 US servicemen, 23 American civilians and 5 foreign nationals had come home through Clark²⁵⁴. On 29 March 1973 the last US combat and support troops left South Vietnam, and MACV was disestablished. A Defense Attaché Office under Major General John E. Murray, with a few dozen military personnel and civilian contractors, took over the management of American military aid. The United States still maintained air power in Thailand and the Seventh Fleet offshore, and continued to bomb in Cambodia. The question of Americans missing in action, about whom Hanoi gave little information, would keep the war alive in American politics for two decades.

#### Case–Church and the end of American combat in Indochina
*Jun – 7 Nov 1973 · Washington; Cambodia · changed from history · EV000-86*
*Initiative: neither*

In June 1973 Congress passed the Case–Church amendment, which barred any US military activity in or over Vietnam, Laos and Cambodia without congressional approval from 15 August 1973²³¹. Until that day the B-52s went on bombing Cambodia, which had continued since the Paris Agreement, in support of Lon Nol's forces around Phnom Penh. Historically on 6 August a B-52 dropped its bombs on the town of Neak Luong on the Mekong, killing about 137 people and wounding about 268, because its navigator had failed to set the offset for the radar beacon on which the strike was aimed. In this war no beacon was used: the J navigated and released on its own GPS, and the strike that night fell on its intended box in the forest east of the river. Neak Luong was not bombed, and the American ambassador in Phnom Penh did not have to pay its families a hundred dollars a death¹⁷⁶.

On 15 August the bombing of Cambodia ended, and with it the last American combat missions in Indochina. The Js went home to the Strategic Air Command's alert pads; Sorrell's cell at Tan Son Nhut, which had been folded into the Defense Attaché Office's intelligence branch in March, was disbanded in September, and its folders for every firebase and district town in the South went to Clark Air Base and then to the Defense Mapping Agency. The Joint General Staff asked to keep a coordinate cell of its own; there was nothing for such a cell to send coordinates to, since no aircraft the VNAF flew could carry a guided bomb, and the request was not pursued¹⁷⁷¹⁷⁶. On 7 November Congress passed the War Powers Resolution over Nixon's veto, limiting the President's power to commit forces to hostilities without Congress. Nixon's promise to Thieu of a forceful response to a major violation was now beyond the President's power to keep without Congress, and Congress would not give it. The sanctuary constraint, which had shaped the war since 1964, now covered the whole of Indochina and was written into law.

#### Resolution 21 and the North's rebuilding
*Oct 1973 – 1974 · Hanoi; the Laotian panhandle and the western Highlands · historical · EV000-87*
In October 1973 the 21st Plenum of the Party Central Committee in Hanoi resolved that the revolution in the South must continue by the path of revolutionary violence, and that the PAVN should respond to Saigon's operations and prepare for a decisive offensive. The PAVN rebuilt through 1973 and 1974: it improved the strategic road along the western side of South Vietnam east of the Truong Son, extended the fuel pipeline into the South, built up stocks of ammunition and armour in the base areas and grouped its divisions into army corps, the 1st Corps in October 1973 and the 4th Corps on 20 July 1974 among them²⁵⁵. It was the North's own decision, on its own reading of the American political collapse and Saigon's weakness, and it was taken while Saigon still held the initiative in the countryside.

#### Saigon declares the war resumed; the Paracels
*Jan 1974 · Saigon; the Paracel Islands · historical · EV000-88*
In January 1974 Thieu announced that the war had resumed and that the Paris Agreement no longer bound his forces²³¹. On 19 January a Chinese naval force defeated South Vietnamese ships in the Paracel Islands, and China took control of the islands²⁵⁶. The United States did not intervene. The clash showed how isolated Saigon had become: its ally would not act, and a great power on its northern horizon was willing to take territory from it.

#### The aid cuts and the shortages
*25 Jan – 9 Aug 1974 · South Vietnam and Washington · changed from history · EV000-89*
On 25 January 1974 supplies of ammunition and fuel to all ARVN units were cut because of budget limits. US military aid fell from about $1.1 billion in fiscal year 1974 to an appropriation of about $700 million for fiscal year 1975, the July 1974–June 1975 year, the figure given by source as $700–750 million²⁵³. Major General Murray of the Defense Attaché Office warned that cuts in support could roughly be equated with loss of territory, and that aid of $600 million would mean writing the South off²⁵⁶. The ARVN rationed artillery rounds, grounded aircraft and helicopters for lack of fuel and parts, and cut patrols; the soldiers' pay lost most of its value to inflation, and desertion rose. The economy suffered from the world oil shock of late 1973 and the loss of American spending.

In this war the ration fell on an army that had been built for ten years around the belt-fed gun and the 30-round magazine, four years longer than the historical one. The Joint General Staff's directive of February 1974 limited each infantry squad to its basic load for a month's operations, and the squad gun went first: about 200 rounds a gun for a month, which is what an M249 fires at the rapid rate in two minutes. Battalion commanders who had learned their trade breaking contacts with the squad gun's volume found that the 1974 ration let them do it about twice in a month, and the advisers' successors at the Defense Attaché Office reported that the infantry, rather than fight with aimed rifle fire as their fathers had fought with the Garand, avoided contacts it could not break with volume. The marksmen, whose match 7.62mm had a separate line, were rationed harder still; by the summer about half the M110A2s in I and II Corps were in arms rooms for want of ammunition. Divisions began to hoard linked 5.56mm in their own ammunition points against the ration, against JGS orders, and by the end of the year the 1st, 2nd and 3rd Divisions held about 9 million rounds outside the corps depots between them¹⁷⁴. On 9 August 1974 Nixon resigned over Watergate, and Gerald Ford succeeded him²⁵⁶. The President who had given Thieu written promises was gone.

#### The ARVN's last offensives
*12 Feb – 2 May 1974 · Tri Phap, Dinh Tuong Province; Svay Rieng, Cambodia · historical · EV000-90*
In the spring of 1974 the ARVN still had the strength to attack. From 12 February to 4 May it struck the PAVN base at Tri Phap in Dinh Tuong Province in the Delta. From 27 March to 2 May, in the last major ARVN offensive of the war, III Corps attacked the PAVN base areas around Svay Rieng in Cambodia, claiming more than 1,200 PAVN killed against fewer than 100 ARVN²⁵⁶. They were the work of an army still able to win local battles when it had the ammunition and air support to do so. They did not change the balance, and after the spring the ARVN was on the defensive everywhere.

#### ◆ The war of attrition of 1974
*16 May – 10 Dec 1974 · The Iron Triangle; Thuong Duc, Duc Duc and Phu Loc in I Corps · changed from history · EV000-91*
*Initiative: enemy*

From May 1974 the PAVN took the initiative with a series of "strategic raids" designed to grind down the ARVN and test its reserves. In III Corps the PAVN 9th Division attacked Rach Bap in the Iron Triangle from 16 May; the ARVN retook it only in November. In I Corps a PAVN regiment overran An Hoa and attacked the district town of Thuong Duc from 18 July; the Airborne Division held the heights around it in a costly victory that lasted until November. At Duc Duc, from 18 July to 4 October, the ARVN 3rd Division suffered 4,700 casualties. From 28 August to 10 December the PAVN seized hills near Phu Loc that closed Phu Bai airfield to traffic and cut Highway 1, and the ARVN recovered them at heavy cost. In 1974 South Vietnam lost about 30,000 killed; the US estimated about 61,000 PAVN and VC killed²⁵⁶.

The weapons the ARVN carried in these fights were better than the PAVN's, as they had been since 1964, and it mattered less than at any time since. On the ridges west of Thuong Duc in September the 1st and 2nd Airborne Brigades took and retook Hill 1062 against the PAVN 304th Division's 66th Regiment; the Airborne's marksmen killed the 66th's spotters and gunners on the facing slopes at 500–700 metres, and its squads held the crest against three night assaults with the squad guns rationed to a belt each for the night. They held, and they bled, because the PAVN's 130mm guns and 122mm rockets were not rationed and the ARVN's 105s and 155s were¹⁷⁴.

They bled in a second way that history's Airborne did not. Since An Loc, Kontum and the Lam Son bases the ARVN's I Corps had been taught, by success, to hold a besieged position and let the bombs break the siege. At Thuong Duc the district town's garrison, the 79th Ranger Battalion, dug in and waited, as its officers had seen the 23rd Division wait at Kontum, for a siege box that no longer existed; the 304th Division's regiment dug its trenches to the wire in eleven days without a bomb falling on them, and the town fell on 7 August. The Airborne's counter-attack on the heights was planned by officers who had held Lolo and Sophia in 1971 under the Js, and its first assaults on Hill 1062 went forward in the expectation that the PAVN's positions on the crest would be broken from the air first; the VNAF's A-37s and F-5As, flying in the monsoon cloud with unguided bombs, could not do it. I estimate that the Airborne Division's losses in the heights were perhaps a tenth higher than historically, a judgment I hold loosely, and I keep the 3rd Division's 4,700 at Duc Duc and South Vietnam's dead for the year at about 30,000²⁵⁶¹⁷⁶. The ARVN, unable to replace its losses or its expended ammunition, had committed its strategic reserve, the Airborne and Marine Divisions, to static defence in I Corps, and had nothing left to move.

The officer reforms made the attrition of 1974 more legible and no cheaper. The battalion and regimental commanders who fought at Thuong Duc, Duc Duc and Phu Loc had been educated on a doctrine in which the infantry finds the enemy and the fires destroy him, and from January 1974 the fires were rationed; their reports to the Joint General Staff in the summer, collected by the Central Training Command, are the most exact account anywhere of what the ration did to each kind of fight, and none of them could change it. At Duc Duc the 3rd Division's battalions, under board commanders, held their companies together through 4,700 casualties with fewer men lost to desertion than history's, and held no more ground⁶³. I keep South Vietnam's dead for the year at about 30,000²⁵⁶.

#### The fall of Phuoc Long
*12 Dec 1974 – 6 Jan 1975 · Phuoc Long Province (Phuoc Binh and Song Be), III Corps · changed from history · EV000-92*
*Initiative: enemy*

On 12 December 1974 the PAVN 4th Corps under Hoang Cam, with the 7th Division, the new 3rd Division and independent regiments including the 141st, 165th, 201st and 271st, and two armoured companies with 14 T-54s, attacked Phuoc Long Province on the Cambodian border. It was defended mainly by Regional Force battalions, reinforced by the 1st and 2nd Battalions, 7th Regiment and the 1st Battalion, 9th Regiment of the 5th Division and the 81st Airborne Ranger Battalion²⁵⁵. The province chief's plan for the defence of Phuoc Binh, drafted in 1973 by officers who had served at An Loc, was a plan to hold the town for six to eight weeks while the bombers broke the siege; it named the bomber cells by the call signs Hollingsworth's men had used in 1972. On 13 December the province chief asked III Corps for "the B-52 support provided for in the plan", and III Corps forwarded the request to the Joint General Staff, which forwarded it to the Defense Attaché Office, which had no means to answer it¹⁷⁶.

On 18 December, in the meeting at the Independence Palace where Thieu and the Joint General Staff decided not to send the general reserve to Phuoc Long, the request was on the table, and the decision taken was historical: the Airborne Division was committed in I Corps, and Thieu would not risk a second division in a province he judged he could not hold without American bombers. In this war the judgment was put in those words, and with more conviction, because the Republic's officers had seen at An Loc and Kontum what American bombers could do for a besieged town and knew exactly what they were not getting¹⁷⁶. The last ARVN post fell on 6 January 1975, the first provincial capital lost permanently since the Paris Agreement. The ARVN lost more than 1,000 killed and 2,444 captured; the PAVN about 1,300 killed and wounded. Saigon did not try to retake it. Washington made no military response²⁵⁵. In Hanoi, where a strategy conference sat from 18 December to 8 January, Phuoc Long answered the question the leadership had set itself: the United States would not return²⁵⁶. The plan for a two-year campaign to end the war in 1976 was adapted to strike in the Central Highlands in 1975. In Saigon the answer was received at the same moment and in the same words, and it fell on an army whose defence of every besieged town had been built for a decade around the aircraft that would not come.

#### The fall of Ban Me Thuot
*10–12 Mar 1975 · Ban Me Thuot, Darlac Province · historical · EV000-93*
For Campaign 275 the PAVN Central Highlands Front under Lieutenant General Hoang Minh Thao, directed by the chief of the general staff, General Van Tien Dung, massed the 10th, 316th and 320A Divisions with the 3rd and 968th Divisions, tanks, artillery and anti-aircraft units. Diversionary attacks on Pleiku and Kontum, false radio traffic and local rumours drew the attention of the II Corps commander, Major General Pham Van Phu, who discounted repeated warnings and kept his forces in the north. At 02:00 on 10 March the PAVN attacked Ban Me Thuot, defended mainly by the 53rd Regiment of the 23rd Division with Rangers and Regional Forces; the town was in PAVN hands by 11–12 March. The counter-attack by the rest of the 23rd Division, lifted in by helicopter, was broken. The PAVN admitted 600–800 killed and 2,416 wounded; the Vietnamese official history counts 28,514 ARVN put out of action, 4,502 killed and 16,822 captured. II Corps lost more than three-quarters of its combat units²⁵⁷. Ban Me Thuot was the strategic surprise of the war's last campaign.

#### The collapse of I Corps
*13–30 Mar 1975 · Quang Tri, Hue and Da Nang · changed from history · EV000-94*
*Initiative: enemy*
On 13 March Thieu ordered Truong to give up most of I Corps and withdrew the Airborne Division towards Saigon as a reserve. On 20 March he ordered Truong to hold only Da Nang; on 29 March he ordered him to turn back and retake it, which Truong refused. The contradictory orders, given as the PAVN 2nd Corps with the 304th, 324th and 325th Divisions attacked from the north and west and the 2nd Division and 52nd Brigade struck in Quang Ngai and Quang Tin, broke I Corps. Northern Quang Tri was lost by 18 March; Hue was surrounded by the evening of 25 March and abandoned; about 6,000 soldiers of the 2nd Division deserted on 28 March; and the PAVN entered Da Nang on the afternoon of 29 March. Soldiers abandoned their units to find their families, and the sea evacuation from Da Nang became chaos, with people fighting to board the ships. I Corps had about 134,000 men against more than 75,000 PAVN and VC; three ARVN infantry divisions and the 147th Marine Brigade ceased to exist, and about 58,722 soldiers were captured at Hue. The PAVN took 129 aircraft, 179 armoured vehicles and 327 artillery pieces²⁵⁸. The best corps in the ARVN, under its best commander, had been destroyed without a decisive battle.

In this war the PAVN's security troops who entered Da Nang on 29 March went first, by a list prepared in Hanoi, to the I Corps regional office of the Special Branch and the Quang Nam PIOCC. The destruction plan of 1968 had been rehearsed every quarter for seven years; on 28 March the regional chief, who had sent his family to the docks that morning, did not order it, and the clerks who might have done it on their own had gone to find theirs. The Quang Tri and Thua Thien centres had burned their files on the 18th and 25th; Quang Nam, Quang Tin and Quang Ngai did not, and the regional office held district extracts of the national file for all five provinces with about 40,000 names and about 11,000 photographs¹⁷⁰. That the collapse would deliver the files with the corps was the risk the memorandum that created them had named.

#### ◆ The retreat from the Highlands
*14–25 Mar 1975 · Pleiku and Kontum to the coast along Route 7, through Cheo Reo · changed from history · EV000-95*
*Initiative: allied*

On 14 March at Cam Ranh, Thieu ordered Phu to abandon Pleiku and Kontum and pull II Corps back to the coast to defend the populated regions. The chief of the Joint General Staff, General Cao Van Vien, warned against Route 19 because of the French disaster at the Mang Yang Pass in 1954; Thieu chose Route 7, a disused road in poor condition. The retreat was ordered verbally and kept secret, even from many of the units and the province chiefs; within days some 400,000 civilians had joined the columns. The PAVN ambushed the column near Cheo Reo, where fighting ended on 19 March, and shelled it along the road. Only the 6th Ranger Battalion and the 19th Armoured Cavalry Squadron arrived in good order; an estimated 155,000 civilians were killed or abducted on the "convoy of tears", and RVNAF air strikes hit civilians too²⁵⁷. It was the decision that lost the war in the shortest time: II Corps, which had held the Highlands through every offensive since 1965, was destroyed on the road in ten days.

In this war the decision had a plan behind it, and the plan was not used. In November 1974 a seminar of the Command and General Staff College at Dalat, under Colonel Bui Quang Trieu, who had gone to its faculty after Quang Tri, wrote a study for the Joint General Staff titled "The Defence of the Republic on Reduced Resources". It concluded that with the aid then voted the RVNAF could not hold the whole country against the forces the North was building, and proposed a withdrawal from Kontum, Pleiku and northern Quang Tri over about three months of the dry season, before any offensive, with routes secured in advance, the families of the soldiers moved first, the province chiefs told, and the Airborne and Marines rebuilt as a reserve. Vien sent it to the palace in January 1975, after Phuoc Long, and Thieu did not answer it. On 14 March at Cam Ranh he took its conclusion and none of its method: three days instead of three months, a secret verbal order instead of a staffed movement, Route 7 instead of a secured road, and the families left to find their own way⁶³. The retreat went as told above. The best piece of staff work the reforms ever produced became, in the hands of a President and a corps commander no board had chosen, the warrant for the worst decision of the war.

#### ◆ Xuan Loc
*9–21 Apr 1975 · Xuan Loc, Long Khanh Province, northeast of Saigon · changed from history · EV000-96*
*Initiative: enemy*

On 9 April the PAVN 4th Corps under Major General Hoang Cam, with the 6th, 7th and 341st Divisions and tanks and artillery, attacked Xuan Loc, the road junction northeast of Saigon on Route 1. The town was held by the ARVN 18th Division under Brigadier General Le Minh Dao, with the 1st Airborne Brigade, armoured units, Rangers and Regional Forces, and air support from Bien Hoa and Tan Son Nhut. The 18th Division held for twelve days against repeated assaults; on 12 April two BLU-82 bombs were dropped on PAVN troops, reportedly killing about 200²⁵⁹.

In this war the 18th Division fought at Xuan Loc with what was left of a decade's equipment and, by April, with the ammunition the Joint General Staff could send it from the Long Binh depot, which by then was most of what remained in III Corps. Its squads had their squad guns and the belts to feed them for the first time since January 1974, because Dao drew everything Long Binh would give him; its marksmen held the edges of the town against the 341st Division's infantry crossing the rubber and the open ground east of the town on 9–11 April, and the 43rd Regiment's account of the first three days records the PAVN assault battalions going to ground at 400 metres under fire they could not locate. It held for twelve days, as it held historically²⁵⁹. The ammunition that might have let it hold longer had gone in March, in the divisional hoards at Hue, Da Nang and Qui Nhon that were abandoned when I Corps and the coast collapsed: about 9 million rounds of linked 5.56mm and most of the match 7.62mm in the country, captured intact¹⁷⁴.

The PAVN changed its approach, bypassed and isolated the town, and on 19 April the Joint General Staff ordered the withdrawal; Xuan Loc was abandoned on 21 April, and the 3rd Battalion of the 1st Airborne Brigade was destroyed in the withdrawal at Suoi Ca. The PAVN claimed 2,036 ARVN killed or wounded and 2,731 captured and admitted 460 killed and 1,428 wounded; Americans estimated about 5,000 PAVN casualties and 37 tanks destroyed²⁵⁹. I do not adjust these figures; the squad weapons raised the price of each assault and the PAVN answered, as it did historically, by not making more of them¹⁷⁴. On 17 April Phnom Penh fell to the Khmer Rouge. Xuan Loc was the ARVN's last stand, and showed what a well-led division could still do; it bought twelve days.

The 18th Division's battalions at Xuan Loc were commanded by officers the board had chosen and its regiments by colonels it had promoted, and Dao himself had been one of the list's names in 1972. In this war there were more divisions like it: the 22nd's stand on the coast in late March, the Airborne brigades at Phan Rang, and the 25th Division's battalions on Route 1 west of Saigon fought in the same way, and none of them changed the arithmetic of 1975, because the corps above them had already gone⁶³. Xuan Loc held for twelve days, as it did historically²⁵⁹.

#### Thieu resigns; the last governments of the Republic
*10–28 Apr 1975 · Saigon and Washington · historical · EV000-97*
On 10 April President Ford asked Congress for $722 million in emergency military aid to rebuild some of the destroyed South Vietnamese forces; Congress would not vote it. On 23 April Ford told an audience at Tulane University that the war was "finished as far as America is concerned". On 21 April Thieu resigned in a televised address denouncing the United States for abandoning his country, and handed power to Vice President Tran Van Huong. Thieu left the country a few days later. On 28 April Huong handed the presidency to General Duong Van Minh, in the hope that Hanoi would negotiate with him; it would not. On 28 April captured A-37s flown by defecting pilots bombed Tan Son Nhut²⁶⁰.

#### The fall of Saigon
*29–30 Apr 1975 · Saigon · changed from history · EV000-98*
*Initiative: enemy*
By late April five PAVN corps, Group 232 and the 1st, 2nd, 3rd and 4th Corps, with 15 infantry divisions and three tank regiments, encircled Saigon in the Ho Chi Minh Campaign. Rockets and artillery hit Tan Son Nhut on the morning of 29 April; two US Marine guards, Corporal Charles McMahon and Lance Corporal Darwin Judge, were killed, the last American servicemen killed in action in Vietnam. At 10:51 Operation Frequent Wind began: Marine and Air Force helicopters lifted 395 Americans and more than 4,000 Vietnamese from the Defense Attaché Office compound, and 978 Americans and about 1,100 Vietnamese from the embassy roof and grounds, to the ships of the Seventh Fleet. Ambassador Graham Martin left on a helicopter at 04:58 on 30 April²⁶⁰. Thousands of Vietnamese who had worked with the Americans were left behind.

At 10:24 on 30 April Minh announced an unconditional surrender. PAVN tanks crashed through the gates of the Presidential Palace, Tank 390 through the main gate, and at 11:30 a soldier raised the flag of the Front over the palace²⁶⁰. The Republic of Vietnam had ceased to exist. Saigon was renamed Ho Chi Minh City; Vietnam was formally reunified as the Socialist Republic of Vietnam in July 1976.

In this war the Central Intelligence Organization's building held the national Phung Hoang file, about 240,000 names with photographs and fingerprints for about 90,000, consolidated there since 1971. The destruction plan for the building required the order of the Director General of the CIO, who left by the embassy's helicopters on 29 April; a section chief and two clerks burned the index and part of the photographic file in the building's courtyard on the morning of the 30th and were arrested in it that afternoon. The PAVN's security troops, with the Ministry of the Interior's southern section behind them, had the rest of the file by evening. It is not possible to say how many of the men and women sent to re-education camps after 1975, or kept there longest, were sent because their names were in it; the camps' own records are not available, and the victors had other lists¹⁷⁰. It is possible to say that the file held the names of every informant, centre officer, PRU member, Chieu Hoi rallier and selected adviser's counterpart of eleven years, that it was the most complete record of the Republic's friends the war produced, and that this headquarters built it.

#### How the phase ends
